[[Note: The following table of contents is not the final text because rationalisation and editorial corrections are pending]] We enter the SPECIFIC FACILITY ORGANIZATION Parliament's decisions: debate and decision on proposals for revision of provisions of the Constitution, in accordance with Rules 110 of the Constitution and 119 of the Rules of Procedure of the House. Ladies and gentlemen, I have the honour of announcing to the House that our meeting is being attended by the upper Western theories, after having been toured in the exhibition of the Chamber "VENIZELOS" and informed about the history of the building and the way this House is organized and operated, fifty-seven students and students and four educational sessions from the 13th General School of Peristeri. The House welcomes you. [[Applause from all sides of Parliament]] Ladies and gentlemen, in accordance with Rule 119(5) of the Rules of Procedure, the proposals for revision are discussed in a proportionate manner with the provisions of Rules 95 to 104 of the Rules of Procedure, namely the ordinary legislative procedure and the debate refers exclusively to the need to revise and to the revised provisions. The subject of today's debate is the proposals for revision submitted by the SYRIZA Parliamentary Groups, the New Democracy, in particular the report by the Revision Committee, and two more proposals by Members. The report is available to those Members who want to take it. With regard to the organisation of the debate, in accordance with the decision of the Conference of Presidents of 16 February 2019, I propose that two meetings, one on the principle and the revised provisions be made available. Two meetings - I make it clear - without interruption from morning to night, so that we do not have any further debates on the hours and all the meetings needed for the issues. In particular, at the first meeting, that is to say, all members of the Review Committee will be placed as follows: The six of them will be given the floor as the rapporteur and the special speaker with twenty minutes of speaking time. The remaining forty, of course, if they want to talk forty, they will speak for ten minutes each. The listing order has been nominated by the Parliamentary Groups by letters. After the speeches of the forty-six in total - possibly forty-six I repeat - members of the committee, we will enter a second circle of fourteen speakers with proportional representation of the parliamentary groups as follows: Five from the SYRIZA, three from the New Democracy, one from the other four parliamentary groups and two independent MPs, who will also speak for ten minutes each. The second sitting, tomorrow, will be made available for the speeches of Parliament's representatives for twelve minutes each and the chairmen of Parliament's groups with the time laid down in Rule 97 of the Rules of Procedure. Ministerial speeches will be held at the two meetings with speaking time for the relevant ministers in the twenty minutes and for the other Ministers in the ten minutes. Then a roll-call vote will be followed by a roll-call vote on the revised provisions. The lists, and after we have arranged for them to be at the Conference of Presidents at 12.45 p.m., we will give them to all our colleagues tomorrow afternoon so that the groups can take care of them in peace. We have said tomorrow at 8 p.m. tomorrow evening to vote, that is to say that all colleagues will bring them. The procedure will take place in two stages, namely the committees responsible for tomorrow's vote and a first vote will take place, but the next day from the competent services of Parliament in the presence of Members and we will give the result on Thursday at noon, because you realise that there are many articles and that something will not happen. But we won't be late tomorrow night. The 22.00 ́ we've said can be the ultimate limit to close this process.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-02-12-S1-commons.u91	Thank you very much, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, every constitutional revision is of a historical nature. By definition, constitutionality as well as in the context of constitutional and revisional function determines the nature and functioning of the constitution, but also the living constitution, the way in which the Constitution is applied in practice. This Review has an additional historical dimension, because it coincides with - and signals - the country's return to European regularity. As a return to normality, I would first like to call upon the full recovery of our country's democratic sovereignty, after a critical period that has not tried the people and the country only economically but also institutionally. And this return is also characterised by a change in our country's international image by a debtor state to a state of protagonists of developments, with a leading role in his region, not expansively, but rather as a force of peace and stabilisation, as was clearly seen by the Ambassadors' agreement. Of course, return to normality doesn't mean returning to the past. That is why the Constitution is not a neutral text. The Constitution is a glimpse in which social struggles are reflected with a particular social profile. We therefore want this revision to have a stigma, a progressive political sign. And what does that mean? It means that we must take a stand on the one hand on the crucial political problems, on the other, in the dilemma in which Europe is facing and responding by strengthening democracy, while at the same time rejecting nationalism and neoliberalism. It must also intervene remedially in the institutional injuries that have brought to the body of the Constitution and the legal order the period of the memorandums. Because yes, it is true that the Constitution did not bring the crisis, but it was one of its first victims. And there was indeed a paradigm during which our country had reduced its democratic sovereignty, where we were imposed on us rules which were contrary to the fundamental provisions of the Constitution, particularly to social rights, but also to aspects of the expression of our democratic popular sovereignty. So the first direction of the Constitution, where we want to intervene and strengthen it, is democratic. And that means full recovery of popular sovereignty at the parliamentary level, but also strengthening of the citizen through institutions of direct democracy, complementar and not competitive towards representative democracy, which will enable him first - first to be sovereign not only in four years, but every time his active intervention is needed. And, secondly, it must be more effective counterweight to the efforts made to overturn the democratic balance of the civil society. The second dimension we want to strengthen is the one concerning rights, the liberal and social nature of our Constitution. Because our Constitution is not only a rule of law, but it is also a social state and the democratic principle is complemented in harmony with these two fundamental principles of the constitution. What does that mean? It means that we should strengthen individual rights. Discrimination on grounds of sex and sexual orientation is a classic example of the need to strengthen protection not only against vulnerable groups, but also against crucial identity issues for our society. But above all, to strengthen individual and social rights. Their ability to restore a decent standard of living for all people. That is why we are proposing specific ways of guaranteeing this protection, through universal services to which all citizens will have equal access. It is the modern way, the European way, for the operation of the welfare state. There are some of our flagship proposals, among those that I have mentioned. The introduction of a mere analogy is one of them, precisely because it strengthens popular sovereignty by allowing the most reliable reflection of the will of the people in the composition of the House. It is precisely because we are interested in popular sovereignty being exercised effectively, that we complement these proposals with other proposals concerning, for example, the constructive vote of censure, the possibility of Parliament being able to show firm governments. And because we want to strengthen the democratic nature of politics as well. ParlaMint-GR_2019-02-12-S1-commons.u97	Ladies and gentlemen, the process of constitutional revision is indeed Parliament's dominant process, with which not all Parliaments are subordinated precisely because of the strict nature of the Constitution and the revising inertia that exists between the revisions under Article 110. On the contrary, in view of this fundamental difference in our proposals and proposals from the SYRIZA, the SYRIZA proposal shows a proposal which demonstrates that nothing has been forgotten and nothing has been learned from the previous crisis, which has, admittedly, led to some delay in the country in de facto bankruptcy. Because, you know, the question isn't why we went bankrupt. If we want to be honest with ourselves, above all, the question is why we are so late to go bankrupt! And now that we have learned and suffered all of this from bankruptcy, the proposal of SYRIZA colleagues to propose a 'happiness' Constitution, a constitution which, in the name of neologism of regulatory density, is verbally strengthening with aggressive definitions and contempt for existing and guaranteed rights, such as health, welfare, work and insurance, and, in fact, the crisis has taught us that social and individual rights - which are in Article 25, Article 22 - are supported by other constitutional provisions on social and individual rights, by constitutional provisions and parameters found in Chapter 6 of the Constitution, which relate to the country's fiscal substance and budgetary power. If we do not strengthen the constitutional provisions governing financial counter-reactions to social rights, then social rights will be like the decorated praetors and high people who have gone to welcome the barbarians to the square, who have not finally come, and the poet is asking about the praetor "why do they get precious bats today, with silver and hair, extraordinaryly carved". Ladies and gentlemen, we do not need precious clubs with silver and wool in the Constitution. We just need to note that since the country has collapsed budgetaryly or for budgetary reasons, the Constitution can and must protect the country from the collapse, not to be neoliberal, but to be social and to be able to protect social rights in health, work, insurance everywhere, a state of alkimo budgetaryly and not a state of paralysed and a state that is prone and portrayed to return to the perpetual fiscal practices of the past. This is the main difference in the proposals: A Constitution for Responsibility, with a broad and bold revision proposed by us, or a demagogy constitution, a working constitution for your election struggle and your post-election despair, to effectively prevent the revision, if you finally send anaemic proposals, which will give a thought to the next majority on whether it is worthwhile to carry out a revision of one, two, three articles or not to wait a little while and to make an extensive revision, which will include all the constitutional requests that will help the country develop out of crisis and remain mainly outside the crisis and which are in chapters with which the SYRIZA proposal has not dealt with, in other words, chapters, education, development, justice, the environment. The proposal of the New Democracy, therefore, without being "extremely carved with silver and minced" is landed and realistic and aims to improve the functionality of the three-seat state. The three-stop state is the state that legislates, we, the state that governs, the government, and the state that is judiciary, justice. And these three forms of state, its three functions, must, in the constitutional spheres which concern them, based on past experience, be improved, in order to have more effective administration, more rapid administration, and not control over its leadership, and to have better legitimisation procedures in this House, without the end of ever-increasingly urgent bills, the clandestineness and barrage of amendments. No constitution, even what will include all the necessary changes in its provisions, can in itself lead the country forward if the majority chosen by the Greek people, who are sovereign, and the government does not address it with due respect. We limit the reasons for the early dismantling of the House so that the elections are normally held every four years at regular intervals. These three parameters form the tripartite part of our proposals concerning government stability, which I have described. ParlaMint-GR_2019-02-12-S1-commons.u103	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the Constitution is a crystallisation of the historical political context which requires its revision. Therefore, this Review will record the historic moment of the rot and decline of Greek political life. Although the Constitution is considered to be the supreme law of the State, since all other legislation governing social life derives from it, it is nothing more than a record of a country's political and social and economic forces as recorded in the Charter. These forces, when they are not the product of revolutionary creation of the Constitution, to record in a majority way, are often called into question or lead to illegitimate constitutions which, although legally strong, live a little and are overthrown. The role of a constitution, not in the past, but today, is not a panacea. The constitutions, in the present sense, are human creations of the political context in question, which are subject to dismantling or briefings or less briefly through which the people come out, the only legitimisation that empowers the leader, who is deemed worthy of history, to express the strong will of the people and of his nation. Our story is rich in such constitutions. Although there were many regional or local constitutions, the first national-wide constitution, however, which established Greek society as a necessary criterion for political legitimacy, was Greece's provisional constitution that was passed at the First National Assembly in 1822 and included a hundred short paragraphs. The Constitution of Epidaurus, known as being revised in 1823 by the Second National Assembly, known as "The Law of Epidaurus", to follow in 1827 the Third National Assembly "The Political Constitution of Greece" in Troisena. But the leader - and if the Greek people listened to this - who suspended the operation of this last Troizina constitution directly invoked the difficulties that this charter had put into his work and proposed that it should be abolished in 1828, a year immediately after it was established, he was the greatest Greek governor, the greatest Greek politician in modern history, Ioannis Kapodistrias. Kapodistrias founded the "Panellenon" and later the "Germany", i.e. two advisory bodies which he used to ratify the decisions he took. In fact the Kapodistrias gathered all power in his hands. A first category is amendments that do not aim to adapt obsolete constitutional provisions to modern social needs and perceptions, but instead aim at the constitutional introduction of minority political ideas that do not find legal base in both political order and society. Such, for example, is the proposed introduction of religious neutrality, which creates too many problems, without actually solving anyone and causing the popular feeling in general simply for the benefit of political obsessions of a overwhelming minority audience. A third category is amendments aimed at changing the rules for the operation of the institutions in a way that also serves small and medium-sized interests, effectively affecting the institutions. A fourth category relates to changes which have been made in a rough manner, wrongly misguided, we consider, unclear and without compromising what is at stake. Such proposals are, for example, an amendment to Article 24 on the protection of forest ecosystems, which, inter alia, defines the concept of forests in a way that is obviously arbitrary. A proposal likewise is the direct election of the President of the Republic by the people, which will primarily mean that - and I stress it - a possible future constitutional transition from the presiding parliament to the presidential democracy. Another category - sixth in the order of what I mentioned - are proposals which, regardless of their specific content and their correctness or not, create important areas in the existing Constitution, such as religious neutrality or the establishment of non-state universities, etc. Ladies and gentlemen, the Constitution is the state's statute. By definition, its purpose is to describe in general the main principles governing the organisation and functioning of the State as the rule of law and as a social State and the basic rules ensuring compliance with the above principles. Consequently, the Constitution as the main pillar of the legal order is called upon to be of a universal and time-consuming nature - we all know this and agree to the whole House. Every proposal to revise it must be made in dreadful and on the basis of wide-ranging social and political consensus and compositions, but it must also concern only the above fundamental principles and not extend to specific issues to be regulated by the joint legislator. In addition, in conditions of non-regularity, such as those prevailing in our country in recent years, the constitutional review should not first be abducted because it is in danger of switching from an instrument for adapting the supra-party rule of law to an attempt to limit the work of parliament and government to micro-political criteria. Firstly, the distinction between politics and politics, 'policies and the cultural' in English. Politics consists of our daily practices, from what is being done here in the House, the ministries, the government. The policy, however, refers to the fundamental way in which social ties are organized with its profound contradictions. This is a modern reference to the Roman distinction between autoritas, legalized power, and potestas, the power that maintains the cohesion of society through the domination of the few over many. This dual concept of politics as a consensus and a conflict is also expressed at the constitutional level. The constitutional reason for this is twofold, an artificial language which sets the rules of political and social reality, but it is at the same time a critical political language, the language of social and individual hope, the question of social injustice and inaction. The constitutional reason therefore combines the area of experience and the horizon of expectation, consensus and conflict. Secondly, this combination of consensus and conflict was expressed in the post-war state with what we called social rights and a social state. In other words, a marriage of opportunity between the two main ways in which national wealth is distributed, on the one hand, the market and ownership, on the other hand, social justice. And it was just democracy that controlled uncontrolled market action and corrected its results and social justice. But the end of the Cold War released capital, capitalism from control of social justice. Governments are no longer asking for the recognition of citizens, but for the love of markets. Thus, this new convergence between the Central Left and the Central Right was created, which led to this hybrid of neoliberalism. Today this economic model is in retreat everywhere, throughout Europe and only in Greece we hear from the constitutional proposals of the opposition, the possibility or the desire for this model to continue. Thirdly, the organic complement of this so-called neoliberal convergence is the post-democratic treaty. Societies are complicated, they tell us. Problems are extremely difficult. The class struggle is over. Social tensions have been reduced. Only experts and technocrats have the right responses to the major economic, social and development problems. They should therefore not be consulted and voted on. People make mistakes. Citizens' participation is discouraged. Election abstention is growing and we have the great increase in right-wing and nationalism throughout Europe. These are not problems for the opposition, but for us, they are the centre of the multidimensional crisis that we have been through and from which we are dating. Our proposals re-populate politics in three directions: firstly, representative democracy, secondly, popular sovereignty in its direct democratic form and thirdly, the relaunch of the social state. The most important strengthening of the representative principle is the establishment of the proportional electoral system. The creation of the multi-party coalition and co-responsibility governments is the strongest counterweight to the Prime Minister's model and government almighty. In the same context of strengthening the government's policy and stability, there are proposals that prevent Parliament from being dismantled on the grounds of the failure to elect a President of the Republic. Our proposal therefore tries to prevent elections from taking place, giving Parliament a long time to create the necessary consensus, but recognizing the participation of the people in the supreme state process. In the same direction, it is also the proposal to be the Prime Minister elected MP, but also to propose a limit of terms of terms for MPs to stop the logic of family rule, dynasty and contempt. The second series of proposals is based on the fact that our society is gradually going through the transport era where the general mind, science and technology, language and communication have become key production forces, operate with horizontal cooperation, with intense networks, with network connections, radicals. In such a knowledge-based society, the limits of its delegation and of its vertical political organization are visible. We are therefore proposing to extend popular participation by holding a referendum on critical issues or a draft legislation, as well as the introduction of the popular legislative initiative. These measures bring people to the fore, but they also bring dialogue and consultation to the people. We are told, of course, that this goes against the principles of democracy and the Constitution, but a Constitution as a law which is outlawed by political power and at the same time restricts it, regulates political power, must reasonably come from a higher power. Historically, all the important constitutions, what we call "scheduled power", are the results of revolution, liberation, overthrowing dictatorships. It was the expression, what Habas Seyes called, "educational authority". And this editorial power remains active. It is the substrate supported by the Constitution and is a continuous presence of the people in the exercise of power. Our proposals, therefore, are the beginning of a process of reconnecting democracy with the municipality. At the heart of our programme is, of course, the relaunching of the social state. I will not say much about this, but for us the social state is not some secondary and paradoxical measures, a safety net for our poor fellow citizens, but it aims to transform social inequalities. And here I would like to make one final comment in relation to Article 5, where we propose that discrimination on the basis of sex, sexual orientation and gender identity should be banned. I think that the controversy of progressive and conservative perception is clear. The battle for Greece of the 21st century will be fought on this conflict. I believe that progress will win. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-02-12-S1-commons.u139	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, for the next year's economy I will only develop the part of our party's proposal on amendment - Article 62 and Article 86 of the Constitution. After all, the review of these articles was recommended to the Review Committee as our party's special rapporteur. The proposed amendments seek to restore, on the one hand, the price paid to the political world, especially during the period of crisis and, on the other hand, the people's confidence in the political system and democracy itself. I absolutely believe that, with what I will explain below, you will be convinced that it is possible to achieve our goals and that our proposal will be voted on by a majority of more than three fifths. After all, as has been noted in the debate and voting on our proposal in the Review Committee, this is the most mature thing to be accepted by the above majority. More specifically, with regard to each of the two articles mentioned above: Rule 62, our proposal for the revision of this article relates to parliamentary immunity, in particular to the non-violent Members. Parliamentary immunity is an extension of the principle of freedom of establishment laid down in Article 60(1) of the Constitution and has two parts: the irresponsibleness of Members, established by Article 61(1) of the Constitution and their irresponsibleness, established by Article 62. With our proposal, the impunity, namely the requirement of prior authorisation by the House for the prosecution, arrest, imprisonment or otherwise restriction of the Member, is limited exclusively to offences relating to parliamentary status within or outside the House. The previous leave of Parliament is abolished for all other offences, to which will apply to Members of this House exactly what applies to all citizens. It is essential to point out this point: parliamentary immunity, i.e. both irresponsible and irresponsible, are typical examples of institutional guarantees. That is to say, not in favour of the Member as a person, but in order to ensure the proper functioning of the democratic culture through the unrestricted exercise of the Members' powers. It is for this reason that parliamentary immunity is limited to what is necessary, which is achieved by our proposal. Finally, with our proposal, parliamentary immunity is fully in line with the relevant case-law of the European Court of Human Rights, which, in judging cases concerning parliamentary immunity, found that this should be provided only for acts directly linked to the parliamentary duties of the Member. It should be noted that one of the judgments of that court, namely the judgment of 16 November 2006 in Tsalkidzis v Greece, is of Greek interest and relates to Article 62 of the Constitution. Our proposal for a revision of Article 86 of the Constitution, which regulates criminal liability of Ministers and State Secretarys, in active and former terms, for offences committed in the performance of their duties is: firstly, the abolition of paragraph 2 of the article, which was first adopted - I stress - by revising the current constitution which took place in 2001, and it stipulates that Parliament - which, according to paragraph 1 of that article, has exclusive competence for the exercise of criminal proceedings against Ministers for offences committed in the course of their duties - may exercise its powers until the end of the second part-session of the parliamentary term which starts after the end of the crime. Secondly, the abolition of paragraph 3 of the same article, which stipulates that Parliament has either the right to revoke its decision on criminal prosecution of Ministers or to suspend both the procedure and the main proceedings. Thirdly, the addition of an interpretative statement stating that the offences referred to in paragraph 1 of that Article, namely those carried out in the course of the ministerial service, do not include what was done only when the ministerial duties were carried out. The reasons which, according to our party, not only justify, but make our proposal urgently necessary with the above content are as follows: Firstly, the time limit referred to in paragraph 2 of paragraph 3 within which the House may exercise its powers is amortised and its impermeable status deprives Parliament of its competence, which is exclusive. In other institutions of the State, i.e. the prosecutor, the judge or the judiciary, may not enter directly or indirectly within the scope of this parliamentary competence. The expiry of the above-mentioned depreciation period therefore results in the elimination of the gains, which have the same effect as those of the derecognition. The above provision, with its minimum amortization deadline, actually leads to the impunity of the Ministers for offences which are committed in the performance of their duties and which, due to their nature, have a huge moral indulgence and often cause enormous damage to the Greek public. Parliament's ability to revoke its decision to prosecute criminal proceedings raises strong concerns about the operational autonomy of the prosecutors and the independence and impartiality of the proceedings. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] It is clear that these two provisions are brutally violating the principle of equality and rightly provoke the strong and harsh criticism of the entire legal world and the anger of the citizens. They shatter and undermine their confidence not only with regard to governmental and representative institutions and political bodies, but with regard to the whole political system and democracy itself. With our proposal, then, non-private provisions on the political system are being abolished, and criminal prosecution against a minister will be possible at any time within the framework of the ordinary law of time when the law of limitation of the offence is being lifted, without the possibility of the House either withdrawing its decision to prosecute or suspend the latter. In other words, it will apply to the ministers what applies to all citizens.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-02-12-S1-commons.u171	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, the Constitution is obviously the foundation stone of an orderly and prosperous state. It is the island that continues the rule of law, maintains human, individual and collective rights in full and guarantees its harmony with the existing socio-political conditions, without becoming a subordinate to the current majority. Over the last few decades, the political system in Greece has been experiencing a crisis that has been transformed into a total lack of confidence in the institutions and in the representative system. Our citizens turn their backs and despise the entire state mechanism and the political system, challenging the quality of democracy. Responding to the democratic deficit and improving the quality of democracy is a need and an obligation to which we must respond immediately, and we must not pass it on to the future. The revision of the Constitution is an opportunity to bring about significant changes in the functioning of the institutions and to redefine those elements that promote the re-establishment of our political system. The revision of the Constitution is an important opportunity, so that together with fiscal consolidation and administrative restructuring we can modernise institutions and close the unresolved ones, whose retention in the constitutional text undermine our political system and are a justification for general decency in the face of politicians and the institution in the representation. Such an openness to which the opposition agrees, that the time has come to settle definitively and irrevocably, is the provision of criminal liability for ministers and parliamentary immunity. This serious victim, which introduced the brief special limitation of the Ministers' offences, came from the 2001 constitutional review and for eighteen consecutive years, has brought all concepts of equal treatment before the law. In particular, at a time of intense economic crisis and the deterioration of the political system, the revision of the Constitution can be combined with the beginning of a new political era, where power will be exercised with absolute transparency and not using special prerogatives, which are at the expense of the Greek people. On the question of parliamentary immunity, too, it seems that we have a broader agreement on amending Article 62 of the Constitution. Criminal immunity as a result of the non-violent MPs, in direct contrast to the constitutionally established Article 20(1) of the Constitution and Article 6 of the ECHR on the individual right to legal protection of the affected citizen from whom he holds parliamentary status, has rightly called on all of us to constantly question the entire Greek people. With the revision of Article 86 and Article 62, the quasi-priority regime will finally cease and the principle of equality and equality will be restored, which constitute the fifthity of justice, the rule of law and democracy. The fact that the revision of these two provisions also suggests that the Civic Opposition, with some variations, creates the backdrop for a creative dialogue, which can only leave a positive footprint in the parliamentary history of the place. In addition, another common site with the New Democracy is the prevention of political instability caused by the dissolution of the Parliament due to the non-election of the President of the Republic. Political instability has many times negative and disastrous results for our country. The consent of the amendment to Rule 33, the combination of indirect and immediate election of the President of the Republic by the electorate and the introduction of a majority of eighty Members during the proposal for a revision of our own, creates the guarantees that will effectively eliminate the early dismantling of the Parliament on the occasion of a national issue of exceptional importance, which only had the aim of provoking elections in order for the political opposition to return to power. The Government, while fully respecting the popular will and being a genuine guarantor of it through Article 102(2), introduces into the constitutional text the institution of the local referendum, and Article 44(2) promotes the strengthening of the institution of the referendum, which constitutes the direct representative form of democracy. Only ten resolutions have been adopted since the establishment of the Greek state. Their number and only bear witness to the distance between people and government and, above all, the lack, if not fear, of confidence in the people's will. The adoption of forms of direct democracy establishs the direct involvement of the people in government and their clearer expression of national and social issues. In the recent past, however, the government has demonstrated that the people must be a participant in matters relating to national issues of the highest importance and not a mere observer, to be chosen and expressed when circumstances require this, except for elections, they are no more than a referendum. That is why we are proposing to strengthen the institution by reviewing Articles 42 and 102. The electorate wants and must remain active and the government is not afraid to address the people and to consult them on matters relating to them. He is not afraid of what that opinion will have, and indeed with the proposal for a referendum directly from the people through a request of five hundred thousand citizens on critical national issues or through the request of a million citizens on drafts of critical social issues. The people acquire voice, strength and control over government policy. With regard to the New Democracy's proposal on Article 44, on an accurate and comprehensible way of expressing the question to a referendum, this is correct as a proposal, but particularly vague when we talk about a revision of the Constitution, demonstrating how superficially it is facing the top institution of the referendum and thus the citizens of the country. In conclusion, I would like to stress that our proposal for the revision of the Constitution is a genuinely reformative proposal, with a view to strengthening the rule of law, promoting popular participation in the political life of the country, empowering the institutions of representation and, in general, the political system and protecting social and collective rights, by ensuring a decent level of living, protection of work, the recognition of health as a social right, strengthening collective bargaining, the definition of minimum wages, the mere proportionalness and strengthening of popular sovereignty. The creation of consensus in modern democracy is the most difficult achievement and consequent challenge for all of us to transform our disagreements into fertile and constructive dialogue, with the aim of improving the quality of democracy and the proper functioning of the genuine democratic culture. SYRIZA's proposal has opened up institutional and structural issues. The crystal clearness of the revised provisions we are proposing, in the face of the deliberately unclear proposals of the opposition, makes the need for them to be voted on historically so that Greece can change its time and move on the path of progress, solidarity and development. The fact is that the initial awkward climate of the underlying disposal in the Review Committee has succeeded an atmosphere of consensus, without consensus other than Articles 62, 86 and 94. Today, after eight years of crisis, having the Greek society in an embryonic memorial era, it shows as necessary a fundamental constitutional re-order with clear targeted and innovative provisions, specifying the political thought of profoundly progressive institutional transformation, democratisation and correspondence with social programmes. Our intention is for the country to have a modern progressive constitution, whose visibility in future can clearly and democratically respond to the social and political demands of our country. I invite you to vote on SYRIZA's proposal. Thank you very much.

I'm starting with old times. In the 17th and 18th centuries there was the Enlightenment with its many wings, which changed Europe’s image in substance. This movement of ideas has led to the major urban revolutions, English, American and French, and has also changed politically in this sense the whole framework of previous royal-centered regimes. Then came the Holy Alliance, as we know. What they tried to break on the substance, for the first time on the scale of Europe, with our Revolution of 1821 that relied on the oldest hopes, created the Philatelic movement and brought Greece and its struggle at that time at the heart of the political debate of the time. However, the progress of the liberated, in the way it was made, did not follow the hopes of the struggles of the people in 1821. A foreign monarchy came here because it was thought that the Greeks could not identify themselves, build their own institutions and elect their own supreme rulers. And the country has since followed a long course, some moments with its large parts, but also with many painful periods, such as wars, dictatorships, overthrows of democratic conquests, etc. The climate of these two centuries created, I'd say, a feeling of intolerance. There were hopes that were disproved, there was a feeling that our struggles are at some point leading up, but at the same time some people come along and disprove those hopes. The process of national unification has also been slow. That is, it took about two centuries to complete, as it was completed. In this context, therefore, a wide range of forms of such intolerance have been developed. I mean, we're the ones who we are, we've got the story we've got, others are envying us and others are jealous of us. To God, do not open up to new horizons, do not make real areas that will bring Greece into dialogue with its own specificities, of course, with the rest of the world. That is what I think the Prespecies Agreement is answering. The Prespecies Agreement, if you want to see it in such a historical dimension, what it says is: We do not have problems with our neighbours, we do not have problems with our identity because we have confidence in our identity, we have as a nation, if you want, confidence in our identity and we are open to dialogue and debate with every neighbour of our people, even with those who at times of our previous history have been faced. In other words, the Prespecies Agreement is changing the climate. Because sometimes to change such climates that have long history need real political areas. So the Prespecies Agreement is a real political branch that wants to change this climate. And here are the two issues, the Prespecies Agreement and the Constitutional Review. How do they connect? Constitutional Review we are doing today, I repeat, and only two years later, in 2021, we are celebrating the two hundred years precisely since the Revolution of 1821. The House will finally decide on the revision of the Constitution in the same year, essentially, that we will be celebrating these two hundred years. So in the House of Reform and with the provisions of the Constitution, we must measure how Greece gains its confidence and, in some areas where it lacks, issues of democratic rights at least, the same as the other countries of Europe. Maybe better. And so, finally, because time does not allow it - I will say two words for two of these articles, and I could refer to more. Article 3 speaks of the state's neutrality in relation to religion, without apparently ignoring the contribution of Orthodoxy to the previous history. But the neutrality of the state means not only passively unreserved religion, but also active neutrality, that is, protection of all other doctrines, of all other religions, of atheism and so on. Absolutely related to this issue is the political oath. Because our fellow citizen may become Prime Minister, Minister, or whatever you want, without being Orthodox. This means active religious neutrality. I don't have any more time. Thank you very much.

The New Democracy, invoking the immunity of the independence of justice, proposes a ban on the participation of judiciary officials in the government or in political positions for three years after they have left the House, the choice of the supreme leadership of justice by a parliamentary committee among older judges, the reform of disciplinary authority to judicial officers. I shall be brief on them. The judicial inconsistency provided for in Article 89 of the Constitution is intended to defend the objective impartiality of the judges by preventing the development of relationships which may give rise to conflicts of interest. In that sense, the above article provides for projects adopted as being incompatible with the capacity of the judiciary in order to ensure the unaffected performance of his duties in all respects. It is clear that in the case of retirement there is no need for the critical condition for the protection of which the incompatibility, that of the unaffected performance of the duties has been established. Therefore, the New Democracy proposal for an extension of three years after the retirement of the judiciary is unjustified and we do not think it should be adopted. The power, now, of the Minister of Justice to exercise disciplinary action against judicial officers is justified by the need to balance public functions. The proposal of the New Democracy implying the abolition of the disciplinary authority of the Minister of Justice and the introduction of the absolute disciplinary self-control of the judiciary actually tends to abolish the balance of institutions and to create an essentially ruthless authority. It should be noted here that the powers of disciplinary action are only the starting point of disciplinary control. The substance of the case shall be judged by the disciplinary board composed by a majority of senior judicial or regular judges, while the lower judicial officers shall be deemed to be hierarchical. Therefore, this regulation does not mean that judicial independence is affected. Now, with regard to the different choice of the supreme leadership of justice, many proposals have been put forward by the parties and by the judiciary associations without any agreement between these proposals. Indeed, the current way of choosing the supreme leadership of justice has not produced the best results. However, there has not been an integrated proposal that could work better. We believe that, in this particular way of choosing the supreme leadership of justice, the principle of separation of powers and the personal and functional independence of judicial officers could be ensured. When? If it is ensured, i.e. if it is respected and the principle of legality is respected. If justice respects the principle of legality and does not grant it for the sake of feasibility, any vulnerability points in the current way of choosing the leadership of justice shall be balanced. The recent case of NOVARTIS has raised problems in dealing with the principle of legality by politicians and I am explaining. Greek legislation in 2011 introduced the prosecutor's institution and inquisitor to corruption in order to provide special know-how and technical assistance to investigate large corruption cases, while in 2014 the institution of the protected witnesses of the public interest was introduced. Both of the institutions that I referred to in this case were slandered and slandered by the politicians who adopted them. Judges as well as witnesses were slandered, sued and threatened. I am finishing on this matter with the fact that as long as legality declines in favour of feasibility, no way of choosing the supreme leadership of justice does defend the personal and functional independence of judicial officers. I shall oppose our disagreement with the proposal for Article 94, the abolition of the procedural prerogatives of the public, as well as the proposal to strengthen the Court of Auditors' audit powers, because there is no need to amend the powers of this court to the detriment of its jurisdictions, because it will alter its nature. ParlaMint-GR_2019-02-12-S1-commons.u196	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, the Revision of the Constitution must meet two parameters. The second parameter is linked to the requirements of the juncture, because the revision is not done and is never promoted in vacant. The context is that the country has come out of the memoranda and strict supervision after many years of crisis and memorial policies. On the basis of these parameters, the revision of the Constitution serves the need for changes in the face of critical decisions to be taken both in our country and in Europe that will determine their future. I will focus, on account of time, on three issues which, in my view, identify the progressive nature of the SYRIZA proposal. They concern strengthening democracy through the expansion of public participation, the reconstruction of the social state through the enrichment of social rights and the safeguarding of regional cohesion through the conduct of specific policies for the less-favoured regions of the country, mountainous and island regions. Today democracy, which operates under globalisation, in an environment of rapid technological change and the concentration of wealth in the hands of a few, shows signs of fatigue. They are constantly being removed from the field of democratic political controversy. At the same time, various entities, such as some independent authorities, often impose policies that are even presented as a one-way or a necessity. In addition, principles included in the Constitution appear in practice empty content, lacking substance. The principle of popular sovereignty is limited to the election of representatives either for Parliament or at regional and municipal level. This is because the wider environment does not favour the essential involvement of the people. The legislative initiative belongs to the House, mainly, or rather exclusively to the government, because of the appointed Members in the majority of Parliament. It is therefore proposed that the legislative initiative should also be attributed to the citizens, in such a way as to recognise the possibility for hundreds of thousands of citizens who have the right to vote on proposals for legislation, which will be introduced into this House for discussion, preparation and voting. This reduces the structural deficits of representation, the participation of citizens is not only limited to the rights of election and election, but also extends to the recognition of participatory rights. In this way, the politicisation and participation of citizens in the public are strengthened. This proposal is not an alternative to representative democracy, but an additional one, since the legitimacy of power is strengthened by the active intervention of the folk. In addition, in the same direction, the strengthening of popular participation, both the introduction of the simple proportional system as a permanent electoral system and the proposal for a referendum on a popular initiative and the proposal for an obligation to ratify by a referendum of an international treaty which provides for restrictions on the exercise of national sovereignty. So democracy itself is institutionally strengthened and the representative system itself, and the principle of popular sovereignty becomes essential. Ladies and gentlemen, in contrast to individual freedoms, social rights constitute an important element of the social state. The introduction of social rights is linked to the fact that the social majority does not have access to basic goods, as the financially powerful are managed and fruitful of the wealth produced. Thus, social rights are the expression of the fundamental right in the life of ordinary, average human beings and aim to ensure that the majority of the social majority participates equally in economic and social life as much as possible. The provision and recognition of social rights are guaranteed rights, but also objective legal principles, which is why they are directed not only at individuals and collective entities, but also at the state which is obliged to act in specific directions and pursue policies with specific content. Indicatively and in this case, the workplaces, social security, education, health and other social goods in which the State is legalised to have the first or dominant role in order to protect the public interest are activated. It makes it imperative that social rights should be given a legal commitment beyond the political declaration. At the heart of SYRIZA's proposal to strengthen the protection of social rights are important interventions which strengthen their binding status. It is proposed that the constitutional guarantee of a decent level of living for all through universal social services and income support be guaranteed. This proposed provision on which a general right to social welfare is founded is supplemented by reference to the special protection to be granted to the family, marriage, maternity, children, young people, the elderly, disabled persons and the needy. In addition, it is proposed that the institutional upgrading of the right to health, which is explicitly recognised as a social right and not simply a state duty to care, resulting from the State’s obligation to provide universal access to effective health benefits. By adding a new provision, it is proposed that public control of basic social goods, such as water, electricity and their distribution networks, should be guaranteed. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] Finally, it is proposed that the articles relating to work, which, during the period of the Memorandum of Understanding, had the greatest influence, resulting in its complete deregulation, should be strengthened. In particular, arrangements are proposed for the general terms of employment and, in addition, for the collective autonomy of free collective bargaining and collective agreements, for the application and unilaterally for the arbitration procedure, for the provision of the principle of more favourable treatment for workers, etc. And I come to something last. It is well known that in our country, ladies and gentlemen, apart from social inequalities, there are also major regional disparities which are mainly reflected in mountain and island regions. These disadvantaged areas due to their distance and difficult access from and from the centre, demographic ageing and their generally desertification are not always easily addressed to general government policies and need special treatment to stay alive. By reducing or eliminating regional disparities, their overall economic and social development can ultimately be achieved. In this context and the proposal for positive regional development policies of a specific nature. For the smooth and uninterrupted functioning of public administration in the mountain and island regions, which require their adaptation to the specific characteristics and conditions of these regions.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-02-12-S1-commons.u202	Ladies and gentlemen, as I have had the opportunity to point out at the beginning of this procedure, in the debate that took place in plenary when it was decided to set up the Revision Committee of the Constitution, this is a rare and top-level procedure which belongs to the Parliament's exclusive competence, individual and individual Members of Parliament who are involved in this process, having, as must, a knowledge of the history and the particular weight of his vote, because the revision of the Constitution is not an amendment to a common law, it is a change in the country's regulatory charter that regulates the crucial relationship between the juncture and history. Because, as we say very often, democracy is periodic, it is timely, it is based on the constant expression of the popular will, but there are restrictions on the expression of that will. In majority voting, these restrictions relate to minority rights, minorities, individuals and social groups. There is, therefore, a legal text that conquers modernity, the Constitution, the strict and written constitution, the product of the two major revolutions of the 18th century, the French and the American revolution, which offers this beneficial guarantee to the functioning of a democratic system of governance and regulates the relationship of democracy and the rule of law with history, with long time. This is all based on the strictness of the Constitution, which is now, with the initiation of the review process, being placed under sect. That is why we need the greatest attention to ensure that accidents do not occur during this transitional period of the Review, which is a period of'record' to the strict character of the Constitution. That is why every constitution of a European, continental, such as ours, lays down very strict conditions for a review of consensus in order to prevent the extreme risk to democracy and the rule of law, which is the risk of constitutional populism, of bias, of mystic perception of the Constitution and its Review. These conditions of the review consensus are, of course, primarily social and political, related to climate, atmosphere and political culture. And, unfortunately, we are going through a period of dramatic and miserable in this respect, a period of decline. We see the problems that exist in the functioning of the House, in the functioning of the institution of the political parties, the problems that exist in the role of Members, we see the unreliableness of those who are going on at the political stage, but in addition we see some people rejoicing about it, some people using parliamentary procedures as a lever to solve micro-party problems. And, of course, I am referring to the governmental majority. That is why, apart from the prerequisites for social and political policies, which unfortunately do not exist, the conditions for legal and institutional matters are important. The Constitution demands an increased majority on the revision of the Constitution, i.e. forced consensus between the parties, between the government and the opposition, presupposes the intervention of the electorate, the test of the present correlation of parliamentary forces before the electorate. It also provides for the participation of two Bulgarians in this process, and there must also be a period of maturity of five years in order not to make unreasonable use of the process of revision of the Constitution. This prevents us from the risk of easy constitutional rhetoric or easy constitutional tactics, from creating to the people, the illusion that everything can be solved through the Revision of the Constitution, that all the gaps, shortcomings, the weaknesses of the political system, the weaknesses of society, the weaknesses of justice, the weaknesses of public administration, the weaknesses of the business world, and even the weaknesses of society itself will be solved by magic through the Revision of the Constitution. Instead of emiting this false and dangerous message, we should now have debated the Revision of the Constitution, following the experience of the last ten years of the fiscal and development crisis, discussing the top issue, which is the resistance shown by the Constitution to major economic, social and political pressures, but also the lack of provisions relating to so-called fiscal awareness, namely mechanisms to warn the rulers, the parliament and the public of opinion about the progress of budgetary matters and the risk of derailment that the people are finally paying him, the weaker are finally paying him, the country's economy is finally paying him, because the nation is losing sovereignty and equality within the European Union and within the euro area. These rules of budgetary awareness do not only concern the government and the legislator, the political institutions, but also concern the judge, who, by exercising judicial control over the constitutionality of laws, must also be aware of the financial framework, the possibilities, the risks and do not think that he can, in a purely legal way, resolve issues relating to the economy and, indeed, the economy at a European or global level, that is, even the attitude of the markets themselves. The Review also cannot be interpreted as meaning that the major consensual 2001 review, which has provided the place with huge regulatory deposits, which are largely still untapped. For example, in the chapter of social, individual and civil rights, in the chapter of group rights and rights of group action, in the chapter of modern threats to the information society, in the chapter of problems of bioethics and technology, in the chapter of the digital age and of digital rights and threats, the Greek Constitution is the most comprehensive internationally thanks to the 2001 revision. In fact, there is no need for anything more in these matters. There simply needs to be economic and financial conditions for implementation, the legislator needs to use the Constitution and the judge should also come to correct and complement the legislator in the direction of protecting rights. After all, we are not talking about reviewing a classic sovereign constitution of the 19th or early 20th century. We are talking about the revision of a constitution of a Member State subject to huge international and European restrictions. Because next to the Constitution is European Union law, International Law for the Protection of Human Rights, in particular the European Convention on Human Rights, alongside the case law of the Greek courts is the case law of the Court of Justice of the European Union and of the European Court of Human Rights, the Constitution itself, national, subject to international judicial control by the Court of Justice of the European Union and the European Court of Human Rights. Therefore, sometimes the national review legislator is vain, because there are international levels of protection of rights, international levels of environmental protection, international levels of protection of economic governance, which the national review legislator cannot violate. On the contrary, the national constitution must be interpreted in accordance with European Union law and in accordance with the European Convention on Human Rights. That is why we have a radical change in the interpretation of Article 14(9) for the so-called 'basic shareholder' in the media, and that is why we also have a radical change in the regulatory content of Article 16(8) of the Constitution to ban the establishment of higher schools by individuals, because European law requires every country - member to operate the other twenty-seven education systems in the other twenty-seven Member States. The debate on Article 16 is therefore an old-fashioned debate within the framework of European Union law and the case-law of the Court of Justice of the European Union on these matters. As you can see, under the present circumstances there is absolutely no point in discussing the revision of Article 110 itself, which has three structural elements: the increased majority, the intervention of the electorate and the time-limit for an hour, which no one can touch, because if these elements are affected, the strictness of the Constitution is affected. There are, of course, issues that belong to the so-called atabism of the Left in Greece, such as relations between the State and the Church. The debate we have had in the Constitutional Review Committee has shown that it is sufficient to make a point of reference in Article 3 on State-Church relations, an interpretative statement which I have proposed since 2006, which will make it clear that prevailing religion is not official state religion, but descriptively the religion of the majority of society, and that Article 3 in no way justifies exceptions or deviations from Article 13, religious freedom and religious equality. ParlaMint-GR_2019-02-12-S1-commons.u233	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, the constitutional review is a process of high democratic symbolism and political importance. The Constitution reflects the function of democracy in the political system, and that is why any revision should in principle take into account modern political and social needs and show the way forward. We have lived - the truth is - a period of unwilling application of the legal and prosperous institutions by the former governments of the New Democracy and of the PASOK. In our country the political system and large parts of society have been tested, to a large extent, especially during the period of the economic crisis and the memoranda. Especially in the first years of the crisis and in the blind implementation of neoliberal policies and austerity policies, the smooth functioning of democratic institutions went to second degree. The technocratic concept that he had led in these years completely alienated the people from his representatives, creating a reversal of parliamentaryism. We are, however, in the face of a new phase in our modern history, where the recovery of citizens' confidence in parliamentaryism is necessary. The country has managed to stand on its feet and the final exit from the people of Memorandums gives new prospects in all areas of social, economic and political life. It is time, now that we have come to the clearing, to look back and to correct in the charter those distortions which have denounced the quality of our democracy and to prevent phenomena that have affected it. Our experience, therefore, in recent years and the new needs that have been created, create an appropriate juncture for deeper changes in the direction of resuscitation of neoliberalism and the armouring of social structures, rights and social justice. Our proposal is structured in four main axes: the strengthening of civil rights, the expansion of democracy, the distinctiveness of the State - Church and the protection of social rights and common goods. Our intention is for the country to have a modern progressive constitution, which will protect public goods such as water and electricity from the rubbish of private initiative, emphatically safeguards the protection of work and workers and strengthens state guarantees for universal access to health services for all citizens. The attack on social rights in the years of the crisis has been ruthless and intent on us, in our proposal, to shield citizens' individual and social rights. The opposition parties must demonstrate greater political courage and be rid of micro-participatory purposes so that the creation of a modern constitution can emerge as a result of wider consensus for the benefit of the Greek people. Our top proposal is the one that includes changes to Article 86 on the responsibility of Ministers, on the abolition of privileged institutions in favour of the political elite, on the way in which the President of the Republic is elected, and on relations between the Church - State where the religious neutrality of the state is guaranteed. These are areas of modernisation and the reintroduction of equality between citizens and policies. No one can seek excuses for not amending the provision on the responsibility of the ministers. If the current constitution is maintained as it stands for eight or ten years and the unacceptable Article 86 is not revised, this will be regrettable for the political world and its credibility. We must not waste any more time. We must root out the client state and protect parliamentarianism. It is time for policy to stop being a place of impunity and covertness and for every citizen to take a step. With regard to relations between the State and the Church, I would like to say the following. The State, the political world, the Church, the citizens, and the believers today have the maturity, the soundness and the sensitivity to accept the rationalisation of these relationships. A wide-ranging consensus can be formed so that we can move on to a new era in the relations between the State and the Church. The time has come to expressly guarantee the religious neutrality of the Greek state in the Constitution and this is an important step towards modernising and liberalising our constitution. At the same time, with our proposal, we are aiming at the proper functioning of the democratic constitution and under Article 54 of the Constitution, we are proposing the application of the proportional electoral system to the parliamentary elections. This excludes electoral systems that over-subsidize the first party, such as the current system, with the so-called 50 seats bonus. Part of this constitutional review is also our proposal by adding a new provision, paragraph 5, to Rule 56, on the establishment of the parliamentary term of office, with the aim of ending the existence of political families who consider politics and the eruption of state-of-the-art attitudes. The aim of our proposal is the greatest possible consensus to proceed with a review - a station that meets the current challenges and will safely lead us to a new era with more democracy, public participation and confidence in the state. Our aim is to put popular participation at the forefront of the political process. The elections must, of course, promote multi-faceted people's participation and help to develop the individual and collective political crisis, but we must create a new political culture by means of collective proposals and by highlighting the dynamics of the people's initiative and collective identities. We therefore propose, in Article 74, that it be amended in such a way as to enable hundreds of thousands of our fellow citizens, who have the right to vote, to submit proposals for legislation which will be introduced into the House for discussion, preparation and vote by strengthening the popular will. Ladies and gentlemen, we all together have to create the conditions and favourable conditions for the next Review House to be able to vote on a Constitution for a modern, democratic, European state. SYRIZA also submitted and supported, as part of the work of the Committee on Revision of the Constitution, proposals with a timely progressive that enhances transparency in political life, the coherence of the social fabric and the rule of law. With our proposal for the Revision of the Constitution, we are proving that we want to further shield democracy and parliamentaryism, individual and social rights, the rule of law that has been affected in the years of the crisis. This is a popular check, and in this check our proposal obeys. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-02-12-S1-commons.u236	We're continuing with Mrs. Jackie, SYRIZA MP. Here, my colleague, you have the floor.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-02-12-S1-commons.u237	Ladies and gentlemen, as of 2013, it has been possible to launch the process of revision of the Constitution after the completion of five years of the previous unsuccessful efforts by the New Democracy, which has not, in fact, succeeded in gathering the necessary consensus and, more generally, the conviction that its revision is pending as a historic matter in our state history for two decades. And that is for two reasons: firstly, because our institutional relationship with the European Union imposes as a historic adjustment the constitutional review, since our Constitution has basically been drafted by a national state, which has then joined a supreme European institution which has suffered and been enlarged and deepened. Furthermore, for some people, there is still the question of whether there are Union regulations that are super-constitutional, that is whether there are provisions that have more legal effect than the Greek Constitution and scientific and political needs to be given the necessary answers. Secondly, because Greece in recent years has been tested by an extraordinary economic and financial crisis that has tried the citizens and continues to test them, but also by the institutions. Therefore, the control of the institutions, in the light of the crisis which has tested them, is a necessary political project. In this light, therefore, ladies and gentlemen, we are now called upon to decide how the guidelines for a successful constitutional review should be. A Western democracy finds its most authentic expression of the effective protection of individual and social rights, such as the right to work, health, social security and the legitimisation of minimum wages, equal pay regardless of age and decent living for all, but also the democratic functioning of institutions and the protection of democracy as such. Why? Because the economic and social crisis has resulted in political extremism and the graphicization of politics, but also in a complete depreciation of politics as a public function. In view of this situation, the revised legislator in Greece of 2019 should have as its main concern to raise the credibility, prestigiousness and price of the political system and this is an existential order of intervention for the proper functioning of the institutions. The political system has lost its contact with society and this can have dramatic consequences for the future of societies. The natural space of the injured policy, the injured public space, is the Constitution, i.e. the fundamental law which defines and organises the effective and democratic functioning of the institutions. Therefore, all the individual interventions made to restore the honor and prestige of the political world have been attempted in their natural space, which is the Constitution. Here is the establishment of a limit for a general term of office, which would lead to a rightful blockade both for the submission of a new nomination and for the election as a Member of any person who has exceeded this limit. We could thus hamper to some extent the creation of a political elite, with professional features of a closed guild, and at a time when politics needs those who do not need politics. Only in this way can familial phenomena be eliminated. Because in the old political system we have no phenomena of hereditary monarchy, but we have a large number of phenomena of hereditary democracy. In fact, five families in the last fifty years have been dominating the political life of our country, having even acquired property rights for the state and altering their members to governmental criminals. The establishment of parliamentary mandates therefore help, but not enough alone. The inner-party democracy must also be established in the parties. In the same direction, the proposal to abolish the immunity of the Member of Parliament on the occasion and not in the performance of his duties, which has so much caused society and has become an element of the dismantling of politics by the populists, has given them the pretext to slander policy towards democratic citizens. Here is the provision for a revision of Article 86 of the Constitution, which gives the sense, not unjustly, of the unjustified privileged criminal treatment of the ministers in relation to ordinary citizens and at the same time gave the impression that a protected pouch has been created. Our proposal for a revision of Article 86 therefore abolishes the short deadline for depreciating, providing that for the crimes of the Minister there is a time when the offence that applies to ordinary citizens is dropped. All the above proposals, of course, for the revision of the provisions of the Constitution concerning civil servants and for the restoration of the credibility and prestige of the political system, must be seen in conjunction with the constitutional provision for the democratic integration of the parliamentary and therefore the electoral cycle, because only then will they guarantee the necessary modernisation and rationalisation of the political system as a whole. It is true, ladies and gentlemen, that the way in which the President of the Republic was elected has until now been an extortion mechanism for the early use of the polls, where his election was falsified as a state matter, while in fact it was a way of circumventing, a way of undermining the four-year government service. Our proposal, therefore, for the revision of Rule 32, now requires an increase of two-thirds of the total number of Members in the first two votes for the election of the President of the Republic, and if this is not achieved, an increased three-fifths of all Members in repeated, if necessary, votes until six months after the start of the election procedure and since none of these votes have been held by the required majority, the President of the Republic is directly elected by the people. This proposal, as it goes without saying, seeks, on the one hand, to avoid the provocation of early elections for political reasons and, on the other hand, to ensure the consensual election of the President of the Republic. In the same direction, of course, other proposals of the Review are being initiated, such as the motion of censure against the government only when a ready-made successor is proposed, so that there is no disruption to parliamentary function and political order, as well as a constitutional provision for the establishment of a more proportional electoral system, which is a constant historical and timely demand of the progressive world in Greece and the West, since it guarantees the most representative political and electoral expression of society, particularly the creation of a political consensus culture on the issues of national life. Coxism and the sloppyness, however, which the New Democracy has faced with the reform process and the neoliberal direction towards which it has proposed that our Constitution should be guided by its proposal to include accounting proposals in the fundamental law of the state and its initial approach to the uprooting of the entire constitutional review process if SYRIZA does not vote for the revision of Article 16 on the establishment of private universities - from which, of course, it has retreated again for reasons of party feasibility - proves rather an acknowledgment of the importance of the historical context that we are currently struggling with and results in a manifest political discomfort and a lack of real proposals and positions on the country's fundamental map, in the most critical historic turning point of the last decades. It is not the last time that the New Democracy consumed and spent the previous review process in 2008 in unnecessary micro-legislative arrangements, which had no impact on society and the political system. However, I would like to end, ladies and gentlemen, with a point of order and a point of order. The finalisation of the constitutional process in two stages, i.e. essentially in two Bulgarians, which ranks our Constitution, established in terms of the reform process, in the tough constitutions, has an interpretative legal and political logic: to confirm genuinely and bindingly by two Bulgarians the historically formed revision will to amend the provisions to be revised with specific regulatory content. If the regulatory content and the review procedure are not specific, it is only about the number and titles of articles in the Constitution, it is clear that the Proposed House may decide in one direction and the Review House should move in the opposite direction. This, however, is a legal absurdum and is not constitutionally tolerated. On the contrary, it exposes the political world and the review process itself, since, for the same reason, the major constitutional issue, which was raised in the Greek people's crisis, in the citizens' judgement in the elections that took place between the two Bulgarians, appears to be two diametrically opposite arrangements. Therefore, ladies and gentlemen, the vote that will be followed does not obviously concern only the numerical titles of the proposed provisions, but above all their regulatory content and regulatory direction. Ladies and gentlemen, it is now realistic to expect from our political system a constitutional review that would help to overcome it. On the other hand, the tendencies of political extremism fuel them not only the Constitution, but also the real pathogens of Greek society. If these can be brought up, among other things, by holding democratically organised parties, by renewing political staff without heirs, donors, etc., then a constitutional review with these characteristics would simply confirm the democracy involved in practice. The issue for our society is to replace today's hierarchical, feudal pyramids with institutionalised political organisations, representing society against power rather than the other way round. It is also a question of ending the reproduction of a semi-closed class of policy professionals and separating public policy from private economic power. Finally, it is a question of making the transition from a super-democratic political system to a new and more democratic model of state and, above all, the institutions. This is the subject of this Constitutional Review. Thank you, Mr President.
