However, the question of the work of the procedure, beyond its ideologicalisation, usually arises or rather arises mainly in what is defined or discussed in Article 110 on the revision procedure, especially as regards the question of the Parliament's commitment to what is decided in this House, namely the proposing one. We must take account of three elements, three words, if you like. Firstly, the need to revise provisions, secondly, the direction of this review and, thirdly, its content, namely, the final content of the provision, the text of the revised constitution. I am referring as an example to Rule 32 on the election of the President of the Republic. In fact, a rumour is spreading that it is withdrawn from the government, perhaps because the government accepts that the content of the proposal does not, as in scientific theory, commit the next House, namely the Review House. Here is the first one, the need for a review by the nominated parliament. Secondly, the direction is also determined. In this direction, let us say, it could be that the procedure for the election of the President of the Republic should not lead to the dissolution of the House, to recourse to the polls and to the early cessation of the life of a democratically elected government. This is the direction, the ratio, if you like, from the proposing parliament. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] Thirdly, the final content of the revised order is formed by the next parliament, which will emerge after elections, the revised Parliament. I shall conclude, Mr President, a little bit of your tolerance. Why should the weight of the final content of the revised provision be in the next House? Because the national elections, ladies and gentlemen, which will take place, will give rise to the provision - the need to revise it will already have been noted by this House - the necessary additional popular legitimacy, which will impose its final review and shape its final content. This is the full process. The principle of popular sovereignty is met, a fundamental principle of the functioning of the parliamentary culture. Two parliaments, the first one proposed, the nominated parliament, the second one revised, the revised parliament and a national election to take place between the two parliaments. This is also the current view of scientific theory, the view of the major constitutionalists, Manesi, Dimitrios Tsatsos and the most active constitutionalists. But you have supported other things here. You submitted the Review to political settlements. In practice, you are undermining the consensus in the review process. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] This work - and I end here - ideologically charged and not ideologically in the Review is not a problematic, a difficult simple review, but it is also a great - I think - lost opportunity for the country. The political system and the political staff must not need similar provisions to protect themselves. It must lay down rules for more moral governance, more diligent choice of individuals, more democratic functioning of parties. In view of the fact that employment with the public is voluntary, service of the public interest by positions of increased responsibility cannot tend to serve the own interests which are "assisted" - in introduction to that word - by provisions such as that of Article 86, i.e. provisions which create a sense of impunity. It is not an occupation for those who perceive it as unnecessarily inappropriate. Similarly, the interpretative statement contained in the SYRIZA proposal, which clarifies that any ministerial offences are exempted from the rules laid down in Article 86. Finally, in the same light, the proposed amendment to Rule 62 of the Constitution for parliamentary immunity should be voted in favour, as it restricts it to the absolutely necessary measure, namely offences directly related to the performance of the duties of the Member. With regard to the unity of "individual and social rights", the proposals of the SYRIZA concern the revision of Articles 21 and 22 of the Constitution. The revision of these articles is absolutely essential because the economic crisis has shown many gaps in the Constitution, the provisions of which must be adapted to the present economic and social conditions. The fiscal adjustments, which the country was forced to make, have primarily hit the workers and the economically weak, both in their incomes and in their ability to access health, insurance and welfare services, thereby increasing the sense of insecurity and lack of protection. SYRIZA proposes to guarantee the state, through universal social services and income support, a decent level of living for all, identified by scientific methods, taking into account the current objective social conditions and specifically protecting family, marriage, maternity, children, young people and the elderly, people with disabilities and the needy. It is a proposal with very specific practices, which is achieved either through universal social services or through income support or through their combination. The proposal shall in any event ensure the right to a minimum guaranteed income by any instrument. The SYRIZA proposes that the State guarantee the right to health and must provide universal access to effective health services through the National Health System. The proposal expressly recognised for the first time as a social right - and not just a state duty to care - the right to health, from which the state's obligation to provide universal access to effective health services while at the same time establishing the National Health System. SYRIZA proposes that all employees, regardless of sex, age or other discrimination, should have the right to equal pay for equal value paid work. This broadens the protection of work, since it will not be possible in future to circumvent equal pay as a result of age. It proposes, on the one hand, the safeguarding of collective bargaining for minimum wages and the right to unilateral recourse by the social partners to arbitration and, on the other, to lay down general minimum conditions for the protection of workers by law, but always with respect to the limits laid down by international conventions and the principle of human value. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] I shall conclude, Mr President. SYRIZA proposes that social security should be a right. It proposes that the State should be required to ensure effective protection against all insurance risks through a single universal coverage system within a public redistributive system operating on the basis of the principles of solidarity and recompense. This proposal is therefore expressly recognised as a social right for the first time in social security. Finally, SYRIZA proposes to add to paragraph 7 of Article 21, as follows: ‘Basic social goods, such as water, electricity and their distribution networks, are subject to public service status and are public control’. The aim is to ensure in basic social goods the continuity of the provision of services at affordable prices and high quality. Thank you.

I think that the citizens are saying - and in the constant contact I have with them - that they want to raise their wages, they want collective labour contracts, which have been done in several branches, even in OAED programmes with allowances, they want to have full access to the National Health System, whether they are insured or uninsured, with well-paid hospitals, with clinics and staff who have already increased and less involved in medicines. They also want a state and a state that is consistent with its obligations towards citizens without discounts, but they also respect the customs and traditions of every place and see the work of certain priests there, but also their role as religious officials. There is no controversy between one and the other. So the provisions which we are proposing make it clear that the political and institutional tops of the SYRIZA on 'the fairness of policy', as has been described by some in the Constitution, are the highest national legal text. These peaks and final proposals have not been made vacant, which is why they are not fireworks, but mature demands, primarily of society itself, such as what I mentioned earlier. They are proposals reflecting the sad experience of the Greek people in the years of the crisis and the memoranda. And that the Constitution may have acted as an impediment or a bulwark for some freedoms and social guarantees, but it turned out that there were serious circumventions and gaps of protection. So in our overall effort to heal wounds, restore injustices and defend the most vulnerable social groups affected by the crisis, in our efforts to strengthen democracy and national sovereignty, we listen very carefully to the needs of society and the movement, which has strongly resisted all these years against neoliberal austerity policies and achieved significant victories. What, then, are these proposals and peaks? Firstly, the strengthening of social and individual rights through the explicit reference to the universal access of the population to healthcare and health, the guarantee of public control of social goods such as water and electricity, the defence of labour rights through collective agreements and collective bargaining. Ensuring the guaranteed income and a decent standard of living for all, protecting life, honor and freedom, without discrimination on the basis of sex, gender identity and sexual orientation, as reflected in all international texts. Also, the religious neutrality of the state, with respect for the distinct roles of State and Church. The establishment of a proportional system in the national and self-government elections, which has already started to release forces and strengthen participation. We are also defending the ceiling for Members' term of office, so that it no longer produces the Greek political system of professional politicians. Of course, the change in Article 86 about 'the criminal liability of the ministers', so that we do not see any more political factors using their position to cover scandals, conflict and corruption in the future. These are the main axes. On this point, however, I would also like to focus on one of our specific proposals, which I have also developed in committee. It is our proposal to explicitly include in Article 102(2), among other things, the possibility of electing and being elected to the self-government elections for foreigners who live permanently and legally in our country. Ladies and gentlemen, after I have made it quite clear that this will be done in the next Review House, because we have also seen the take news that we are talking about these elections, at which point we have reached the Greek Parliament - I want to stress the fact that there are many of our fellowmen who have lived for many years in the country with full legalization documents, with participation in the activities of local communities, with a significant contribution to the development of the country and who have married Greek and Greek women. Despite this substantial linkage and integration of local communities, they do not have the right to co-decide on the administration of their city and thus lack the opportunity to exercise fundamental rights and the obligations arising from this right. Of course, this issue does not concern us for the first time. There was - as you well know - and Law 3838/2010 predicting the participation of foreigners in local government I degree, with specific conditions and differences, but it was considered to be unconstitutional by the Plenary Assembly of the Council of State with a strong minority, which it considered, using the minutes of the 2001 Review Committee, that, on the basis of the provisions in force, foreigners' participation is not excluded, but is equally included. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member hits the warning bell.]] I shall conclude, Mr President. ParlaMint-GR_2019-02-12-S1-commons.u389	Thank you Mr. Ioannis Theocratic, MP Kosanis of SYRIZA. Mrs Theanos Fotio, Deputy Minister for Labour, Social Security and Social Solidarity, has the floor for seven minutes.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-02-12-S1-commons.u390	Ladies and gentlemen, it is the historic moment that we come out of the catalytic crisis for the country and the Greek Parliament is moving on to those constitutional reforms that are needed to restore the credibility of the political system and of the state, which has been raised in the consciousness of the citizens, in order to strengthen democracy and popular participation, which have been affected in the crisis and in order to strengthen social rights, which have been tested and effectively challenged with the monuments. Our proposal concerns only seventeen articles, avoiding New Democracy's constitutional pillowing that has led to two failed constitutional revisions in 2001 and 2008. But there is an article that we do not want to review. It is Article 16 for public higher education institutions, which strictly defines the protection of the public university, which was passed in 1975 by a large majority of the government of Constantine Karamanlis. Ladies and gentlemen, Article 16 has a surplus because, while it stipulates that universities are public, it at the same time prohibits the establishment of private universities. Because the legislator then wanted to shield the public university and exclude scientific teaching and scientific research from market competition and the influence of self-interest and self-interest. Article 16 founded the new Greek public university and contributed to its development and blooming even in the difficult years of the crisis, a bloom that is internationally recognized. It is worth pointing out that the constitutional legislature has placed this article in the chapter on individual and social rights, in order to highlight the nature of education as a constitutional right. However, since the early 1990s, with the New Democracy Government, the struggle to abolish the exclusive public character of the HEIs, when the scientists opened colleges and promised university degrees against money, while at the same time the government promised friends and sponsors of the New Democracy that either they would abolish or detract from the constitutional ban on private HEIs. The second systematic effort of the leash referred to in Article 16 is again made by the Government of New Democracy in 2006-2007, in which case the fight resistance and the multi-month protests of students and teachers led to a shipwreck of the attempted changes and to a naughty retreat of the New Democracy and the PASOK. The public nature of HEIs, ladies and gentlemen, is not a left-wing obsession, but the only way for the free development of science and research, for the dissemination of knowledge and for the free access of the people to it. What for? Ladies and gentlemen, the university by definition produces and reproduces knowledge, as well as the teaching body that manages knowledge and research. Otherwise, it's not a university. I insist on this: otherwise, it's not a university. This is enshrined in the Constitution in two ways: first, the freedom of teaching and research is guaranteed in Article 16, without which the production of knowledge is impossible. Secondly, in Article 16, too, the self-employed, i.e. the university community itself, elects its administrative bodies, chooses its scientific staff on its own criteria, within the framework of the law. The public university, that is, reproduces the same. It can't be appointed from outside. Both of these elements cannot exist outside the public sector in private universities, whether they are profitable or non-profit-making. And I'm gonna talk a little bit about this candy, about being non-profit-making. The private university is run by an owner - whether it is a private company or a non-profit establishment - that is, it is not the university community that runs it. Scientific staff shall be recruited by the owner or institution respectively. The owner or institution shall determine the content of the teaching and direction of the investigation. While, in Article 16, even the establishment of new university departments, it is evident that the establishment of their cognitive objects is by decision of the State, i.e. in the light of the public interest and the social benefit, unlike private HEIs, or the speculative interest of the businessman or the aims of the non-profit institution, as determined by the same. In other words, private HEIs are non-democratic educational institutions and, I insist, they are of poor quality. And finally, tell us, you of the New Democracy or of the PASOK, an institution, private HEI in Europe, which is a source of concern. Tell us what it's all about. Comparison with the private sector of education for the first two steps is unmanageable. You say: 'After there are in the first two degrees private schools, why should we not have an EHS?' Because at the first two levels of education, the school programme and the curriculum are defined by the State. This cannot be done at the EITs, because that is where knowledge is primarily produced. And this production of knowledge is not capable of such interventions, otherwise the production of knowledge itself is cancelled. University education, ladies and gentlemen - and you know it - is very precise. Even if tuition were to be entered in the EITs - which is the other secret pursuit of the New Democracy, which does not say so, but that is the problem - revenues could not cover expenditure. Profits, of course, will be directed at multipliers or a large part of them will have to be financed by the state. And listen! ParlaMint-GR_2019-02-12-S1-commons.u391	Thank you, Mrs. Theanos Fotio. Mr Charalambos Athanasios, Member of the Lesvos of the New Democracy.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-02-12-S1-commons.u392	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, I should like briefly to refer to the procedural limits of the Review, as regards the competence of the two Bulgarians, the present, namely the Parliament proposed and the next, of the review. This first House, in its decision, after finding the need to revise the Constitution, shall specify in particular which of the revised provisions may become revised, not only in an article, but also in paragraph or even in a subparagraph. This decision must include the need, but also a fundamental one, for a general approach to the revision which is, in fact, nothing more than the reason for the need to revise certain provisions of the Constitution. The precise content of the revised provisions, however, belongs to the next House, the second, which is equipped with a revision, namely the exclusive competence to define precisely the regulatory content of the revised provisions that the first Parliament has decided on as such and only in respect of them. In other words, on the one hand, the second Parliament cannot decide to revise any provisions other than those which the first Parliament, on the other, has decided, on the other hand, with a recent people's mandate to regulate and on the pending issue of the Review, to decide at its first meeting on the content of these provisions, without being bound by any decision of the first Parliament, of this House. I shall set an example. Let us assume that Article 32 of the Constitution states that the announcement of the parliamentary elections should be disconnected from the election of the President of the Republic. That is the elementary direction. If he does not say so, then the second House may not go that way and the provision will be more stringent. Likewise, the election of Vice-Presidents of the Supreme Courts. Why do we say that? We are talking about this in order to guarantee the personal independence of the judges. If we do not say so - if only we remain in the necessity of changing Article 90(5) of the Constitution - then it is possible for the second parliament to say: 'A, the plenary of the legal associations will decide' or only the Prime Minister. These are theoretical examples, extreme, but that's the way it is. So you realise where independence goes.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-02-12-S1-commons.u393	Just to understand, if the first House says so, the latter commits itself to the extent that you have said.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-02-12-S1-commons.u394	It is committed in the direction, but not in terms of content, that is, that it will move in a context. It is an elementary direction that is not binding on content.

Secondly, the first House decides on the necessity of the revised provisions, while the second is freely made up of their regulatory content. I believe that the burden falls in the second House, because this is the Reform Parliament, with the recent popular mandate, which will specifically define - I stress it - the precise content of the revised provisions. Finally, with regard to paragraph 6 of Article 110 of the Constitution, which prohibits a review before the five-year period passes, this will have a basis and must therefore be interpreted and amended. It must be stated that this provision applies only to the provisions that have been revised. In other words, when there is a decision or when public opinion or society has accepted a change - let us say to private universities - we cannot commit ourselves for a whole five years. This space may not be five years. Depending on when the Review will take place, it can be seven and eight years. So the next House cannot, before that time, bring for discussion a provision which had not been revised. This restriction should therefore only apply to the provisions that have been revised and not to those which have not been put into effect. ParlaMint-GR_2019-02-12-S1-commons.u410	Thank you very much, Mr President. Since the moment is historical, I would like to make a special reference, since in my region we have the honour of linking ourselves historically to the first Constitution of Greece. Over ninety years have passed since the Third National Assembly of Troisena, in which the first definitive constitution of the free Greek state was decided, the most liberal and democratic for its time. The Convention, seeking to give the country a definitive city, inspired by democratic and liberal ideas and, of course, by the Greek Republic of the King, declared the new constitution for the first time the principle of popular sovereignty, enshrined the distinction of the state's powers and entrusted it to the parliament, the executive to the governor and the judiciary in the courts. It has properly protected the freedom, basic rights and equality of citizens and has banned slavery, torture and the retrospectiveness of laws. Today, in different political conditions, conditions of peace, national independence and democracy, but counting many wounds after a period of suffocating budgetary committee and after a dense political period of claim and change, we are at a central moment for democracy. The importance of today's debate is reinforced by the fact that, throughout Europe, democracy is threatened by the rise of extreme right-wing perceptions. That is why strengthening democracy is interlinked with the resurgence of neoliberalism. Our proposal is set out in four axes that broaden the main axes already introduced by the first joint constitution: Enlargement of democracy by establishing mere proportionality and the limitation of parliamentary immunity and term of office. Discretion of State and Church. Introduction of institutions of direct democracy to strengthen the role of the citizen. Strengthen the protection of universal public services and social rights. Today the introduction of mere proportionality is essential for the revival of the political system. After a series of elections, which resulted in governments of cooperation, there is an urgent need to develop a culture of cooperation and consensus on the basis of programming agreements. Besides the need, it is also our duty to give each vote equal value by abolishing the system of the first party's premium. This is why the right to vote and to stand as local government institutions is being proposed for foreigners with permanent residence in the country. It is a heavy obligation to restore the absolute value of human life and dignity, physical integrity at this very last political moment, to talk about protecting basic social goods: health, education, social protection and the environment. Against attitudes that they want in the context of the free market to become the social goods north of the capital, we propose a fundamental constitutional guarantee for the social state. We propose that the state should guarantee a decent standard of living for all, as well as the universal access of all people to effective health benefits. We are proposing to guarantee public control of basic social goods, such as water and electricity, so that their distribution networks are subject to public service and are subject to public control. We propose the constitutional recognition of equal pay for equal work and the prohibition of the qualification measure to deal with the consequences of the strike. Of course, religion is guaranteed, but rationalization is needed in relations between the State and the Church and the liberation of political life from religious criteria. In this direction we are proposing that the political oath be established for the President of the Republic and the Members. We want a state that protects religious freedom and worship and is not bound to the religious feeling of a particular part of society. SYRIZA's proposals stem from the experience of the crisis. It is a proposal to strengthen democracy itself. At this critical state time, we should all stand up to circumstances and defend values over time. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-02-12-S1-commons.u413	Thank you, Mr Georgios Georgioda, MP Kilkis of the New Democracy. Mr Christoforos Vernardakis, Minister of State and MP of Athens, has the floor for seven minutes.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-02-12-S1-commons.u414	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, I think there are four key questions that arise in connection with the constitutional review. Question first: Is a constitutional review necessary today? The political system, which was established and established after '74, has already exhausted all the promotional dynamics involved in the 3rd Hellenic Republic since the mid-90s. Since the mid-90s, policies to reduce the State's regulatory role and to increase the privatization of state policies have led to a reduction in the concept of public social good and democratic social contract. At the same time, they were accompanied by increasing political dependence on economic interests. The state of structural authoritarianism has been intensified by the imposition of the memorial class policies, by the symphony of the old partisanism with the major economic interests, as well as the corruption that has come to become a methodology of the country's governance. Therefore, without the Constitution of 1975 as such being responsible for developments, the new reality does not cease to require a deep re-orientation of the regulatory frameworks of public interest, as well as the modernisation of the basic institutional functions. It is also necessary to remove those paraconstitutional rules that especially in the years of the crisis have emerged strongly, taking advantage of either the gaps or ambiguities of the 1975 Constitution and subsequent revisions. Question 2, is there any need for political consensus to revise the Constitution, or is consensus a dynamic process that has a historical maturation? I am referring indirectly to the previous speaker. The 1986 Review focused on the lifting of the powers of the President of the Republic, proposed and imposed unilaterally by the then ruling party, and indeed in a context of great political polarization. However, it has created a acquis and has never been called into question since then even in subsequent revisions. The 2001 Review was carried out in a completely consensual context between the two major parties at the time, PASOK and the New Democracy, and took up a very large number of articles in the Constitution. However, it did not touch on the core of institutional functioning, it did not leave, as the New Democracy itself admits in its explanatory statement, any institutional footprint was a revision of a zero sum. Finally, the 2008 Review, also in a consensual institutional framework, was limited to secondary issues, leaving also an institutional footprint. So what does the review process show? It shows that when consensus is a supposedly technical governance, it is unreasonable and pointless. The institutional union is productive and dynamic when it puts the main political and social dilemmas and the main strategic differences at the centre. Consensus is not an ideological regulatory procedure that can be controlled by legal formalism. That is why the review we are debating today will prove the most powerful and serious review process that has ever been done, because there is a wide difference between the political forces, but through this difference what has been revised it will have resulted from strong and real convergence and not from institutional micro-educations. Question 3: What are the major differences, as recorded in the positions and proposals of the parties? The main difference lies in the direction to be taken by public regulators in the years to come. Our view is that the radical upgrading of the social state and the recognition in the Constitution of State guarantee through universal social services and income support at the level of living, the strengthening of universal access to the National Health System, the safeguarding of social security as a single universal coverage system, the definition of the social goods of water, energy, networks, the integration of new rights into the Constitution, such as those relating to disability, the strengthening of labour rights and collective bargaining is the way to go. On the other hand, we are in the direction of the smaller social state and the smallest possible regulation. The view expressed throughout the New Democracy proposal is a view that is far away from even the liberal model that the state must intervene by regulating inequality of interest when these inequalities may be painful for state or social cohesion. The most clear case in the New Democracy proposal is the constitutionalisation of control mechanisms against fiscal derailment, i.e. the constitutionalisation of the budgetary cut at all levels of the'state organisation', as stated in its explanatory statement. The other characteristic proposal concerns the revision of Article 16, which proposes freedom to establish private universities, which affects the core of equal and open access to a public asset, such as the provision of education and education. Question IV: Are there any of these consensual changes which will give impetus to the country's institutional political modernisation? Although the proposals conflict two completely different ideological choices for the future of our country, there are also mature convergence points, which is the present Review very substantial. The first convergence relates to the disconnection of the election of the President of the Republic from the dissolution of the Parliament. Let us make it clear here - I am opening a parable here - that our own constitutional proposal has as a central thread the strengthening of the role of Parliament and of Members as a decisive decision-making centre, which is reflected in the constitutionalisation of electoral laws and in the constructive motion for distrust, but also in the duty of the Prime Minister not to be a non-parliamentary. A second convergence relates to the overtime change in Article 86. Finally, a third convergence relates to Article 101A and the selection of administrations to the independent authorities. Ladies and gentlemen, one final comment. The constitutional review process is central to the country's deep institutional modernisation. But it is parallel and must be organically linked to the country's national plan for productive reconstruction, with the development strategy. In fact, it's the twin tool that puts the country in the new memorial era. Productive reconstruction and Constitutional Review by common denominator of the emergence of work at the main advantage of the country are the fields that will define the new era. Thank you.

Ladies and gentlemen, climate change can no longer be a marginal issue or be a part of the country. Article 24 proposes the introduction of a provision for constant care and action by the State to address climate change, its negative effects and, of course, to protect biodiversity. Also, developments in new technologies - the fourth industrial revolution is at the door - require that we do not remain without a constitutional provision for the future. In particular, we are proposing to add an explicit reference to the need to take actions to ensure, on the one hand, human rights and a fair distribution of the wealth produced in the modern environment of technological development and the implementation of artificial intelligence. It is the bioethics issues that need to be brought in and anticipated in this Review. For self-government we propose redefining the general competence of local government bodies and recognising the principles of proximity and subsidiarity, introducing explicit reference to specific responsibilities of self-government, social policy, health services, development planning and anticipation of specific resources or tax items in the context of fiscal decentralisation, the possibility of submitting legislative proposals to Parliament and the pilot implementation of self-government arrangements before universal application. I therefore propose that we leave internalisation and turn to successful world practices, which will enhance university education as a whole, increase knowledgelessness, for the benefit of, of course, public universities, society and in particular, young people. I come to Rule 32(4) concerning the disconnection of the election of the President of the Republic from the life of the existing Parliament. This is a proposal for a Review, which is clearly the result of recent history, that is at the end of 2014, of a period of blackening of parliamentaryism, with irresponsible accusations of Members' money, toxicity and extreme polarisation. It was the time when the election of the President of the Republic was used by SYRIZA at the time, for the government to fall, having as a vehicle the president's election. It is therefore a question of stability in political life. Already, we see that the political insecurity and the long-standing pre-election period have a direct impact on the economic environment, with fears and reservations about the country's progress towards openness on all sides. So let us take a step forward towards the future safeguarding of political stability, disconnecting the election of the President of the Republic from the lifetime of the House in question. I would say, of course, that this proposal does not seem to me to be working enough and it is time-consuming, with the six months it provides for. However, I would also like to refer to Rule 24, which does not go far enough in the review proposal. Article 24 concerns the environment and its protection. I cannot understand the non-revision and reinforcement of this article, with the aim of dealing with climate change, the management of water resources and the strengthening of renewable energy sources. Personally, I consider the protection of the environment to be the highest priority of a modern state, which is unfortunately being abused in Greece over and above time. Article 86 on the responsibility of Ministers or - if you like - on the non-responsibility of Ministers, as it had become, and as was actually used. I am positive with regard to the abolition of paragraph 3 for Parliament's prosecuting period for ministers and for the suspension of prosecution. To stop the ministers and the MPs, as provided for in Rule 62, from getting away from justice. As for every citizen, the same should apply to the elected people. That is the only way to begin the gradual withdrawal of suspicion - justified, I say - of citizens against politicians. I am also in agreement with the proposals concerning the setting-up of two committees of inquiry per period, at the initiative of a minority of Parliament, namely 2/5. This is Article 68. I am also in agreement with the non-reservation of parliamentary immunity, except for offences directly related to the exercise of parliamentary duties. Article 62. I will vote in favour of the revision of paragraph 2 of Article 5 to guarantee freedom, without discrimination of sex, identity of sex and sexual orientation, beyond nationality, race, language and religious or political beliefs. In other words, this is not a question of freedom of expression and personal freedom. Ladies and gentlemen, it is important to point out that the constitutional review will not solve the problems of the country. The Constitution is the legal shield, the legal basis of the country and the institutions of democracy. However, the work to be done, especially by politicians, is in the field of the economy, public administration, society and justice. It is here that the political world should put all its powers in place and prioritise the treatment of small and large corruption, bureaucracy, the delay in the administration of justice, which sometimes reaches the limits of veto, but also the challenges of the future, which are already present and are mainly mentioned in the speed and evolution of the new technologies. The Internet did not go through any Ministerial Council and no government in the world. It was developed by engineers and computer scientists. Politics has recently been seeing that it is usually behind developments and that it attempts, in retrospect, to regulate or intervene in new realities, which it does not understand. Bioethics and biotechnology are also areas that change the individual's position in the complex and globalised economic and social environment, in ways that the citizen does not understand nor can he or she formulate nor at all. Here too, politics comes second. Ladies and gentlemen, I would like to see a more bold, more invasive, smarter and effective review of our constitution, which frees forces and shields the principle of universal access for citizens to the goods of the common effort, with obligations and rights, as is appropriate for every citizen. Therefore, the next House, the Review, will have a lot of work, as it will be the one that will give concrete direction and content to the provisions that this House proposes. In order to achieve this, therefore, we need consensus, clean mind and responsibility from this House. Thank you.
